The wing is accessed by a hallway with a marble floor and a 17.25-foot-high (5.26 m) ceiling.[^cite_note-Brown_1966-400] Inside the main level of the wing is a ballroom (originally known as the Susan Edwards Wagner Ballroom), the design of which is based on that of the [Lyman Estate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lyman_Estate) in [Waltham, Massachusetts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Waltham,_Massachusetts).[^cite_note-nyt-1966-09-07-226][^cite_note-Brown_1966-400] Gracie Mansion's ballroom measures either 48 ft × 25 ft (14.6 m × 7.6 m)[^cite_note-Herzig_1966-402] or 50 ft × 24 ft (15.2 m × 7.3 m) across and has a [coved ceiling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coved_ceiling) measuring 18 feet (5.5 m) high.[^cite_note-O'Neill_1973-18][^cite_note-nyt-1966-09-07-226][^cite_note-Stern_p._125-403] The walls of the ballroom were originally decorated with [French windows](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_windows) and gray-blue walls, topped by a [frieze](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frieze) with garlands and fruit bowls.[^cite_note-nyt-1966-09-07-226] There are also 12 [neoclassical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neoclassical_architecture) columns throughout the ballroom.[^cite_note-Pinard_Bogaert_1998-324] A chandelier from 1783 hangs from the ceiling, and eight lighting sconces are mounted onto the walls.[^cite_note-Brown_1966-400] A portrait of Susan Wagner, painted by [Willy Pogany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Willy_Pogany), was also displayed in the ballroom.[^cite_note-nyt-1966-09-07-226] The ballroom's [Adam style](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adam_style) mantel was salvaged from the [James Watson House](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Watson_House), the residence of Archibald Gracie's brother-in-law Moses Rogers.[^cite_note-nyt-1966-09-282-404][^cite_note-NPS_p._4-391]

Flanking the ballroom are two smaller rooms: a dining room and a reception room.[^cite_note-nyt-1966-09-07-226][^cite_note-Herzig_1966-402][^cite_note-NPS_p._4-391] There is also a serving pantry on the ballroom level.[^cite_note-nyt-1966-09-07-226] The dining room's mantel was salvaged from a house on [Greenwich Street](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greenwich_Street).[^cite_note-nyt-1966-09-282-404] Mark Hampton redecorated the ballroom, dining room, and reception room in the Federal style during the 1980s.[^cite_note-Slesin_1984-279] The basement was constructed with a conference room, an office for the mayor, and another office for the mayor's secretary.[^cite_note-nyt-1965-01-12-178][^cite_note-Daily_News_1965-194][^cite_note-Stern_p._125-403] The conference room was designed to fit at least 30 people,[^cite_note-Daily_News_1965-194] and press offices also occupied the basement.[^cite_note-nyt-1966-09-07-226][^cite_note-Stern_p._125-403]

## Operation

The mansion has served as the official New York City mayor's residence since 1942.[^cite_note-Swanson_2004-339] Visiting official guests may also sleep at the house,[^cite_note-Swanson_2004-339] and numerous mayors have invited their political allies to the mansion.[^cite_note-405] The house has also been used for ceremonies and governmental business.[^cite_note-Wiseman_p._37-270]

Gracie Mansion remained valuable into the early 21st century. A real-estate expert estimated in 2008 that the house could be auctioned for \$250 million,[^cite_note-406] and *[Vanity Fair](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vanity_Fair_\(magazine\))* magazine wrote in 2014 that the house and site were worth an estimated \$125 million to \$200 million.[^cite_note-407] *The New York Times* reported in the late 1970s that the mansion also increased the value of mayors' pensions even after they retired. For instance, although Abraham Beame earned \$60,000 a year just before he left office in 1977, he also received a \$30,000 benefit from using the mansion, making his total pension \$90,000 a year.[^cite_note-408] Due to its role, the mansion has sometimes been described as the "People's House"[^cite_note-Diamonstein-Spielvogel_2011_p._87-62] or New York City's "Little White House".[^cite_note-amNewYork_2017_w415-114]

### Maintenance

The New York City Department of Parks and Recreation has owned the house since before it became a mayoral residence.[^cite_note-Stern_p._46-117] The mansion's upkeep is partially overseen by the Gracie Mansion Conservancy, a [public–private partnership](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public%E2%80%93private_partnership) formed in 1981 under Ed Koch's mayoralty.[^cite_note-Black_p._viii-118] The conservancy was originally established to restore the mansion and preserve it.[^cite_note-La_Rosa_1982-286] By the 1990s, the conservancy also employed a curator and assistant curator for the mansion.[^cite_note-Flynn_1990-300] The Gracie Mansion Conservancy oversees educational programs and events at the mansion.[^cite_note-409] In 2012, it was recorded as having a \$1.7 million annual operating budget, most of which was paid by the city.[^cite_note-CBS_New_York_2013_y164-23] The house is a member of the [Historic House Trust](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Historic_House_Trust).[^cite_note-Historic_House_Trust_of_New_York_City_1989_j3922-290][^cite_note-nyt-1989-06-20-291]

### Public access

Although the mansion was closed for public tours between 1942 and 1980,[^cite_note-nyt-1980-09-25-265] it has often been open to the public since then, even when in use as a mayoral residence.[^cite_note-410] As of 2024, the Gracie Mansion Conservancy provides tours of the mansion one day a week;[^cite_note-411] the tours cover the first floor.[^cite_note-412] The de Blasio family suspended tours of the house between 2014 and 2016.[^cite_note-413] Visitors were banned from taking photographs except in the Wagner Ballroom.[^cite_note-heraldtimesonline.com_2017_t893-395] The public has also been invited to the mansion for special occasions such as annual [trick-or-treating](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trick-or-treating) events.[^cite_note-414]

Besides official business, Gracie Mansion has hosted other types of events over the years. Its first fashion show took place in 1956.[^cite_note-415] The mansion also has hosted exhibitions during its time as a mayoral residence. This included a 2015 exhibit of artifacts relating to minority groups;[^cite_note-416] a 2017 exhibit with World War II–era artifacts;[^cite_note-417] a 2019 exhibit of women's paintings;[^cite_note-418] and a 2020 exhibit on social justice.[^cite_note-Pogrebin_2020-369] The mansion has also been used as a meeting place for clubs such as the Gracie Book Club.[^cite_note-419]

## Impact

### Reception



The Wagner wing as seen from the west

One writer from 1913 said that "there are evidences of its old-time grandeur, when it entertained some of the brightest men of the day in this country".[^cite_note-Erhard_1913-22] Another writer said in 1922 that "no resident of such a mansion could wish to travel or to forsake its cheerful rooms always suggestive of home",[^cite_note-Harvier_1922-74] while *[The Atlanta Constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Atlanta_Constitution)* called it "a worthy example of the solidity and beauty of early New York architecture" in 1927.[^cite_note-The_Atlanta_Constitution_1927-83] A writer for *[The Baltimore Sun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Baltimore_Sun)* said in 1931 that "its neat and happy present brings to mind certain outlines of its domestic past".[^cite_note-420] A decade later, a writer for *[The Christian Science Monitor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Christian_Science_Monitor)* described the site "a most fitting frame for the office of the city's Chief Executive".[^cite_note-Ince_1942-37] The *Christian Science Monitor* writer contrasted its preservation with the planned demolition of [Castle Clinton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Castle_Clinton), which had been approved shortly beforehand.[^cite_note-Ince_1942-37] The *New York Herald Tribune* wrote that no other mayoral residence in the city's history was "more gracious or larger than Gracie Mansion".[^cite_note-New_York_Herald_Tribune_1942a-108]

When the Wagner annex was proposed in 1965, *New York Times* architecture critic [Ada Louise Huxtable](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ada_Louise_Huxtable) described it as "notable for its scholarly and appropriate good taste", compared to the original plan for the house's expansion, which she compared to a suburban garage.[^cite_note-421] After the annex was completed, Huxtable said that the annex "is worth every penny that the city did not spend", in part because of the craftsmanship and furnishings of the interiors, as well as the fact that the exterior neither directly replicated nor clashed with the facade of the original mansion.[^cite_note-nyt-1966-09-282-404] *The Christian Science Monitor* wrote in 1966 that the annex was "of architectural and interior distinction, which reflects warmth and a welcoming sense of hospitality".[^cite_note-Hoffman_1966-227] In 2002, architectural historian Christopher Gray wrote that the annex's interior was "oddly oversized" and that the details in the annex's central hall, although appropriate for the 1960s, "looks like applique".[^cite_note-nyt-2002-05-26-41]

The *New York Daily News* wrote in 1973 that the mansion was "a dignified dowager of early American architecture" that remained a prominent presence on the East River.[^cite_note-O'Neill_1973-18] When the mansion's renovation was announced in 1982, a writer for the *New York Daily News* praised the mansion's "18th century farmhouse look, its human scale, its graceful rooms, its embracing porch and unbelievable (for Manhattan) setting" but criticized plans to convert the house into a cultural showcase.[^cite_note-422] After the renovation, a reporter for the *[Sun Sentinel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sun_Sentinel)* wrote in 1985 that "it is the interior decoration that has literally transformed the house" and that the previous mayors' decorations were "dowdy".[^cite_note-Winship_1985-276] Referring to Gracie Mansion and City Hall in 1987, a *Times* writer said: "For all its rush to change, New York, contrarily, installs its seat of power in two of the oldest, most elegant buildings with the most classical lines in New York."[^cite_note-423] A reviewer for *[The Herald-Times](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Herald-Times)* wrote in 2017 that "Gracie Mansion abounds in architectural and decorative interest", citing the floor and furniture of the foyer as being of particular interest.[^cite_note-heraldtimesonline.com_2017_t893-395]

### Preservation

Gracie Mansion was one of 6,500 buildings in the U.S. that were photographed as part of the [Historic American Buildings Survey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Historic_American_Buildings_Survey) between 1933 and 1942.[^cite_note-424] The [New York City Landmarks Preservation Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_City_Landmarks_Preservation_Commission) considered designating Gracie Mansion as a city landmark in March 1966,[^cite_note-425] and the mansion was designated as such later the same year.[^cite_note-Diamonstein-Spielvogel_2011_p._87-62][^cite_note-426] It was added to the [National Register of Historic Places](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Register_of_Historic_Places) in 1975,[^cite_note-427][^cite_note-Lewis_1981-428] following advocacy from Beame and LPC chairwoman [Beverly Moss Spatt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beverly_Moss_Spatt).[^cite_note-429]

### Media and popular culture

The mansion was depicted in a 1952 exhibit of historic homes presented by the [New-York Historical Society](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New-York_Historical_Society) (N-YHS),[^cite_note-430] and was the subject of another N-YHS exhibition in 1981.[^cite_note-431][^cite_note-Lewis_1981-428] At the end of 1966, after the mansion's Wagner wing was completed, the wing and the original mansion were shown in a half-hour [WCBS-TV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WCBS-TV) broadcast.[^cite_note-432] During Gracie Mansion's 1980s renovation, N-YHS curator Mary Black was commissioned to write a book about the mansion's history.[^cite_note-433]

The mansion has also been the setting of, or filming location for, several fictional media works. The original footage from the TV show *[The Yule Log](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yule_Log_\(TV_program\))* was filmed at Gracie Mansion in 1966 and shown on [WPIX-TV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WPIX-TV) (channel 11).[^cite_note-Stern_p._107-434][^cite_note-435] Several scenes in the 1974 film [*The Taking of Pelham One Two Three*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Taking_of_Pelham_One_Two_Three_\(1974_film\)) depicted the house's exterior and interior,[^cite_note-436] although the interior scenes were filmed at [Wave Hill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wave_Hill) in [the Bronx](3338.md), where one room was redesigned to resemble Gracie Mansion.[^cite_note-437] The movies *[Ghostbusters II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ghostbusters_II)* (1989) and [*City Hall*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/City_Hall_\(1996_film\)) (1996) were set at Gracie Mansion,[^cite_note-Castro_2012-398] as was the 1997 novel *The Puttermesser Papers* by [Cynthia Ozick](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cynthia_Ozick).[^cite_note-438] The mansion and its surroundings were used extensively as a setting in [Linda Fairstein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Linda_Fairstein)'s 2010 novel *Hell Gate*; Fairstein said she had set the novel at the mansion because "it had a history and physical beauty that fascinated me".[^cite_note-439]

### Alleged haunting

De Blasio's wife [Chirlane McCray](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chirlane_McCray) has claimed that there are sounds of doors opening and closing by themselves, creaks from the wooden floorboard, and whispers from Archibald Gracie's daughter.[^cite_note-440][^cite_note-Warner_2022-441] Eric Adams also alleged the mansion was haunted,[^cite_note-442] repeating McCray's claims.[^cite_note-443] Adams also claimed to have seen objects moving around in the mansion.[^cite_note-Warner_2022-441] When a television interviewer asked him if he felt the mansion was cool enough, he responded, "No, it’s not, trust me, .... I don’t care what anyone says, there are ghosts in there, man."[^cite_note-Culliton_2022-444][^cite_note-Warner_2022-441] The alleged haunting is attributed as a possible reason for why Adams did not want to occupy the mansion for extended periods,[^cite_note-445] but others have claimed that it was an excuse for Adams not to move.[^cite_note-Culliton_2022-444] Upon leaving the mansion, Adams warned Mamdani about the ghost,[^cite_note-446] claiming the ghost acts like a poltergeist only to individuals that do wrongs to the residents of the city.[^cite_note-447][^cite_note-Taylor_2026-448] Mamdani did not report any ghosts after moving to the mansion.[^cite_note-Taylor_2026-448]
